Work Board

Every job of the migration in one list, in four queues: who does it, what it waits for, what closes it and which file it carries out.

How the jobs are read from the plan, and the priorities, and the progress journal

Read from execution-plan.json and decisions.json: every record of the register that still asks something is a job, with the manual steps that stand for its question, and every other step of the plan is a job of its own (the OpenTofu steps of one file that wait for the same thing are one job). Steps defined: 331. Progress by kind of step: steps to answer: 88 · changes executable now: 27 · waiting: 214 (48 wait for a value); 2 refused (of 331 steps). 48 of the 57 automated steps (OpenTofu or a script) are free of decisions and manual prerequisites (1 waits for a manual prerequisite, 8 require a design decision) · 0 can run as written · 48 of those 48 wait for a value (project_id, carts_service_task_project_roles, carts_task_execution_project_roles, catalog_rds_plan_name and 28 more) · 9 of those 48 rest on a default you have not confirmed (public endpoint, size derived from the source); a person can start 115 of the 272 manual steps now (7 wait for a manual prerequisite, 67 require a design decision, 83 are not executable as the plan stands); 2 steps of the plan are permanently unsupported (refused); 1 step of the plan has its verification incomplete: it carries critical settings of the source that no check validates, which a person confirms in the draft. Every step of the plan is in exactly one job; 288 jobs hold them. Nothing here has been run or approved. migration/work-board.csv holds the same jobs.

Priority: First is the first required action and the manual work every automated step waits for, Holds steps a job some step of the plan waits for (or a refusal), Later the rest.

No progress journal was given, so no job shows progress. Record what you carry out in progress-template.yaml (the run copies it to review/progress.yaml) and run the assessment again, or pass the file with --progress FILE.

Start here

First required action: Allow the calls of STACKIT APIs that are not generally available — Platform Architect. Allow STACKIT's pre-release API, which may change or be withdrawn: Secrets Manager v1 (release status not stated). 4 steps of the plan name this decision and wait for this answer (the steps that come after them are counted in what an answer frees); allowing the calls lets the plan carry them out and makes the migration depend on that API. If you say no (record a rejection), the calls are not made: the steps of Secrets Manager stay open. Record the answer in the decisions file given with --decisions (decisions-template.yaml).

the default of 2 records nobody has answered: public endpoint, size derived from the source; confirm or change each in decisions.yaml; confirming one accepts what the draft does as it stands: the steps that rest on it then count among those that rest on no default to confirm, no opt-in to answer and no value to supply once nothing else of the three holds them, and as approved once every decision they rest on is answered in the decisions file and no value they read is missing; records are counted here as the register counts them, one for each kind of decision and family; the register lists a record under defaults to confirm only when no step waits for its answer

stages of 57 automated steps: 57 in the draft, 48 free of decisions and manual prerequisites, 0 rest on no default to confirm, no opt-in to answer and no value to supply, 0 are approved (every decision and default confirmed in the decisions file, and no value left to supply)

MAN/data/module.dependencies.module.catalog_rds.aws_rds_cluster.this/write-freeze/dump-restore (1 job)
  • Dump inside the freeze: mysqldump --host=<source-host> --port=<source-port> --user=<source-user> --password --single-transaction --routines --triggers --events --set-gtid-purged=OFF --no-tablespaces --databases <database-1> <database-2> --result-file=<dump-file>.sql. --set-gtid-purged=OFF leaves the GTID statements out, which MariaDB does not take, and --no-tablespaces leaves out the CREATE TABLESPACE statements, which MariaDB does not support for InnoDB. The target instance must be empty before the load (the STACKIT how-to of the restore says so): load the dump with the mysql client, as the user of the instance's service key, into the empty instance: mysql --host=<target-host> --port=<target-port> --user=<target-user> --password --database=<target-database> < <dump-file>.sql 2> load.log, and read every line of load.log. A DEFINER account that does not exist on the target, a collation the target does not know and a database the target does not have are the errors the checks name: each is settled by a person, never by skipping the object. Runbook section: terraform/handover/database-dependencies_catalog_rds_this/data-move.md#final-sync-dump-restore. Decide how private servers reach the internet (outbound IPv4): The knowledge base states no closing condition for this setting; the run closes it when a person decides it as a check of the knowledge base (that) fired asks, and that check's closing evidence or decision is recorded. Where the scan cannot prove what the source subnet reaches, the closing evidence is the route table the subnet follows and its default route by exact references, and for a route to an internet gateway the public IPv4 address of every server in the subnet, or an architect's statement of what the source subnet reaches. Where the source subnet has no internet route, it is an architect's decision that the network may reach the internet through the STACKIT gateway with the outbound limits written as security-group egress rules, a decision that the network is isolated (routed = false), or a documented or observed setting that removes the outbound path of a routed network. Decide how private servers reach the internet (outbound IPv4): A person decides it as that finding asks, and the finding's closing evidence or decision is recorded.

Then: Brings closer once it is done (each waits for more): Dump inside the freeze: `mysqldump --host=<source-host>….		 — —
Dump inside the freeze: mysqldump --host=<source-host> --port=<source-port> --user=<source-user> --password --single-transaction --routines --triggers --events --set-gtid-purged=OFF --no-tablespaces --databases <database-1> <database-2> --result-file=<dump-file>.sql. --set-gtid-purged=OFF leaves the GTID statements out, which MariaDB does not take, and --no-tablespaces leaves out the CREATE TABLESPACE statements, which MariaDB does not support for InnoDB. The target instance must be empty before the load (the STACKIT how-to of the restore says so): load the dump with the mysql client, as the user of the instance's service key, into the empty instance: mysql --host=<target-host> --port=<target-port> --user=<target-user> --password --database=<target-database> < <dump-file>.sql 2> load.log, and read every line of load.log. A DEFINER account that does not exist on the target, a collation the target does not know and a database the target does not have are the errors the checks name: each is settled by a person, never by skipping the object. Runbook section: terraform/handover/database-dependencies_catalog_rds_this/data-move.md#final-sync-dump-restore.

Inside the freeze, export the table retail-store-ecs-carts (module.dependencies.module.dynamodb_carts.aws_dynamodb_table.this) to S3 with the DynamoDB export to S3, which requires point-in-time recovery, and the source does not state point-in-time recovery for this table (the provider's default is off): a request for a table without it fails with PointInTimeRecoveryUnavailableException. Switch recovery on first (a change to the AWS table that this plan does not make) and request the export only after it is on, using aws dynamodb export-table-to-point-in-time --table-arn <table ARN> --s3-bucket <bucket> --s3-prefix <prefix> --export-format DYNAMODB_JSON; keep the manifest-summary.json (its itemCount is the count the comparison uses). Without the export, read the table in full with Scan, which consumes the read capacity of the table, and count the items you write out. Convert the export of the table retail-store-ecs-carts (module.dependencies.module.dynamodb_carts.aws_dynamodb_table.this) into documents of the collection of stackit_mongodbflex_instance.dynamodb_tables with a script of your own: unwrap the type descriptors of the DynamoDB JSON (S, N, B, BOOL, NULL, M, L, SS, NS, BS) into BSON values; make the key id the _id (or a unique index on id); keep numbers in the BSON type that holds them: a DynamoDB number has up to 38 digits of precision and a decimal128 has 34, so a value of more than 34 significant digits cannot be a decimal128 without rounding (keep it as a string, as AWS advises beyond 38 digits, or report it); turn a string set, a number set and a binary set (SS, NS, BS) into an array: a set holds unique values in no order and an array does not, so the application code that relies on set operations changes; plan the secondary indexes global secondary index idx_global_customerId (customerId) as MongoDB indexes on the collection (a local index shares the key of the table, so its index leads with the table's partition key); projections are not carried (a MongoDB index finds whole documents); the item size needs no check in this direction: DynamoDB holds an item of up to 400 KB and a BSON document holds up to 16 mebibytes; the consistency the application needs is its own decision: a read of DynamoDB is eventually consistent unless the request asks for a strongly consistent read, and a global secondary index is eventually consistent only; MongoDB's read concern and read preference are chosen by the application's driver. Drain the queues of the AWS broker module.dependencies.aws_mq_broker.mq (aws_mq_broker): its consumers go on reading them, with no producer sending to the broker any more, until every queue holds no message. Read the depth in CloudWatch: Amazon MQ for RabbitMQ publishes MessageCount (the sum of the ready and the unacknowledged messages) for the broker and, on 3.x brokers, for each queue (the dimensions Queue and VirtualHost; on 4.x brokers the figures of a queue come from the Prometheus endpoint), and Amazon MQ for ActiveMQ publishes QueueSize and InFlightCount for each queue and TotalMessageCount for the broker. On a RabbitMQ broker the management API gives the same figure for every queue: curl -s -u "<source-user>:$SOURCE_PASSWORD" "<source-broker-endpoint>/api/queues" | jq '[.[] | (.messages_ready // 0) + (.messages_unacknowledged // 0)] | add' is 0 when they are empty. An inactive durable topic subscriber (InactiveDurableTopicSubscribersCount) holds messages that only its own client can read: connect it to the AWS broker until it has read them. The plan does not read the names of the queues of the broker
